- The common projection factor, opaque adapted orthogonal coordinates,
  Fubini and arbitrary-set outer-volume bound, and both printed geometric
  estimates on p. 203.
- The Gross and convex Caratheodory product upper bounds for arbitrary
  factors in all natural first dimensions on the defined-product domain.
  Null second factors and zero-dimensional first factors have direct proofs.
- The Gross lower bound from Borel partitions, including finite positive
  first volume and infinite second mass.
- Gross equality for arbitrary factors when the second mass is finite,
  with no measurability or finite-first-volume hypothesis. Finite-rectangle
  comparison identifies the restricted measures on all subsets. Continuity
  below is applied to restricted Lebesgue measure on Borel exhaustion sets.
- Gross equality for arbitrary first factors and Borel second factors,
  including infinite masses. A continuous-image measurability argument
  enlarges the first set inside a Borel hull of its rectangle. This uses
  2.2.13, not an assumption that continuous Borel images are Borel.
- Gross equality for arbitrary factors whose second set has a countable
  finite-Gross-measure cover, including infinite second mass. Cover sets
  may be nonmeasurable; Borel hulls give the required measurable exhaustion.
  General sigma finiteness or semifiniteness is not assumed.

infinity, so the new hypothesis fails as required. This is a local
simplification of the mechanism of.

SCOPE: the complete corrected curve formula on p. 177, for an
arbitrary real-parameter map into any metric target on a<b. Variation
equals the actual upper integral of its counting multiplicity against
normalised H1 plus the right-jump sum over [a,b) and left-jump sum
over (a,b]. Both jumps are independently defined one-sided distance
limsups. Infinite variation and divergent sums are retained; no
measurability, continuity, bounded variation, target completeness or
target separability is assumed. Erratum p. 3, book p. 177 line 23,
requires the upper integral. The separate corrected bibliographic
reference [F12] at book p. 177 line 27 introduces no further numbered
assertion claimed here.

Federer omits the proof. The lower estimate uses first-level crossing
points, scalar distance images and oriented jump bridges; finite
numerical budget makes their positive supports countable. Sum the
endpoint estimate using disjoint open image-measure intervals and
the exact opposite half-open atomic intervals. For the reverse bound,
identify the scalar arc-length atoms with the two limsup jumps without
assuming one-sided limits in the target. Separate those countable
atoms from the singleton-null image-cost measure. The segment
retraction localises the original curve without exterior hypotheses.
Countable discontinuities give actual integrability when the jump
budget is finite; the final equality always uses the upper integral.

MATHEMATICAL COVERAGE: the complete item is assembled as federer_2_9_22.

every continuous dual functional to obtain actual vector subsegment
integrals. Their norm bound and the norm-integral measure bound each

with epsilon/(L+1), and the metric law gives relative norm continuity.
For (4)=>(1), actual scalar derivative integrals transfer to evaluations
of the exact norm derivative; full L1 and all scalar endpoint identities
are precisely the abstract vector integral specification. No derivative
representative or extra integral construction is introduced for this step.

reflexivity and completeness are abstract supplied laws. No external
measure/differentiation/integration theorem or new project axiom is used.

zero-bound conventions, and distinguish the metric supporting extensions.

Progress checkpoint: Federer 2.9.22, locally BV norm differentiation (2026-10-24 UTC)
Switch: RP.2026.02.0~9.1~2026.01, explicitly selected throughout.

PARTIAL ITEM: prove actual strong norm differentiation almost everywhere
for locally BV curves in arbitrary reflexive Banach targets, with all
scalar dual identities on the same conull set. The variation equality,
induced-measure and integral/absolute-continuity extensions remain to be
assembled, so this is not a full item or subsection release.

Prove nested finite interval hulls for a real sequence. A countable dense
sequence in an arbitrary real set covers all but its possible minimum
and maximum by such hulls. Null singleton masses remove these endpoints.
This extends uniform bounds on segments joining the set's points to the
whole set, without boundedness or measurability assumptions.

For nondecreasing s and a diffuse measure nu with nu([a,b]) <= s(b)-s(a),
prove nu(s^-1(A)) <= Leb(A) on every subset A. The interval-hull bound
controls inverse images by target diameter; countable diameter covers
and the internally proved H1=Leb identity extend this to Lebesgue measure.
This is a documented route departure from Federer's open-interval/open-
set approximation. It uses the existing 2.5.10 supporting development
(forward 2.10.7), not an external change-of-variables theorem.

Prove Borel regularity of finite measure sums by intersecting Borel hulls.
The derivative variation measure then has a Borel null hull for any of
its null sets. Its actual absolute-derivative subset integral is zero
there; the proved nonnegative zero-integral test implies the actual
scalar derivative is zero almost everywhere on the original set.
Apply this to monotone s and Leb-null Z: s'=0 almost everywhere on
s^-1(Z). Do not infer that the ordinary inverse image is Leb-null.

Finally factor g=H o s using the actual Banach extension. Outside the
weighted inverse image of H's norm nondifferentiability set, use the
actual norm chain rule. On that inverse image, s'=0 almost everywhere
and the distance/variation estimate gives g'=0. This proves the central
norm-limit theorem and every continuous scalar dual identity, with no
separability or global finite-variation premise.

correction: every left (right) chi covariant measure over G is a Radon

Completed subsubsection checkpoint: Federer 2.7.6, the density relating left and right Haar integrals (2026-10-21 UTC)
Switch: RP.2026.02.0~9.1~2026.01, explicitly selected throughout.
Preceding commit: d2d0961.

The lemma of 2.7.6 (p. 125) is formalised: for a left Haar integral lambda
and a right Haar integral mu there is a continuous positive k with
k(y) mu(v) = lambda_x v(y x^-1) and mu(k u) = lambda(u); k is uniquely
characterised by (2) among continuous functions and does not depend on
the choice of v. Positivity of nonzero monotone invariant integrals is a
supporting result.

Mathematical coverage: normalized monotone finite product families are
specified by consistency, empty-product and adjoining-factor laws,
constructed by commuting coordinate integration, and sealed. Actual
finite-coordinate spaces, surjective projections and injective pullbacks
give the printed marginal spaces. Compactness gives finite-coordinate
control and uniform cylinder density. The glued functional has its actual
uniform-norm bound, extends continuously, and satisfies the Daniell laws.
The infinite product is sealed and characterized by every finite marginal;
uniqueness holds among all continuous extensions of the cylinder functional.
Finite and empty index sets are allowed as documented boundary extensions.

For directed compact Hausdorff inverse systems, no bonding-map
surjectivity is assumed. Finite upper indices and compact intersections
prove existence of compatible families and the eventual-image property.
Cutoff interpolation and a direct lattice approximation argument prove
density of the coordinate pullbacks. Consistency gives their norm bound
on the actual limit and a well-defined glued functional. The sealed
inverse-limit integral is normalized and monotone, has every coordinate
marginal, and is unique among all continuous extensions. These are the
standing normalized monotone hypotheses of Federer's product discussion;
an unrestricted signed inverse-limit theorem is not asserted.

Sources: Federer 2.6.6, pp. 120-121; Kelley, General Topology, Dover 2017
reprint of 1955, Chapter 7 Problem R(a)(ii)-(iii), pp. 244-245, for the
lattice approximation background. The proof uses the order-dual max/min
ordering of Kelley's compact-cover argument. The local Federer erratum
contains no relevant entry for pp. 120-121.

The first example uses actual sealed Lebesgue measure times counting

Federer's factor orientation and allowing the equal positive measures
of A1,A2 to be infinite. Hence F is not a Borel family.

Sources: Federer 2.6.5, printed pp. 119-120 Unrestricted associativity is
false: the actual Lebesgue diagonal over [0,1] times an infinite nonempty
factor has measure zero in one association and infinity in the other. The
proved sufficient hypothesis requires countable measurability only of the
outer factors; arbitrary finite associations retain countable measurability of
all original factors and their ordered coordinates. The binary

`product_measure_is_regular` proves **2.6.2(1)** without countable-measurability
or finite-total-measure assumptions on the factors. `product_measure_rectangle_measurable`
proves the measurability assertion of **2.6.2(2)**. The exact rectangle value,
the section identities in **(3)**, and the iterated-integral identities in
**(4)** remain pending: **2.6.2 is not yet complete**.

The two cylinder lemmas use the rectangle splits in Federer's proof directly
on sequence-cover costs. The covering bounds for the two parts, nonnegative
summation additivity and distributivity give the Caratheodory equality after
taking the cover infimum. Intersecting the cylinders gives measurable
rectangles. This establishes measurability before introducing the book's
repeated-integral class; the implementation records that proof-order variation.
Measurable rectangle unions and the existing measurable-hull attainment theorem
then prove regularity.

`product_measure_rectangle_hull` also constructs the more precise hull used
in the printed proof: for every S there is W containing S, with equal product
measure, such that W is a countable intersection of countable unions of
measurable rectangles. Finite measure uses reciprocal-error covering sums;
infinite measure uses the whole space. The theorem includes actual measurability
of W. It does **not** yet assert equality with the repeated integral rho.
`is_countable_rectangle_intersection` specifies this sequential hull form,
and `product_measure_sequence_strict_approximation` supplies the actual covers.

A null-strip regression proves that an arbitrary subset of A × Y is null and
product measurable when A is measurable and alpha(A)=0, even if beta(Y) is
infinite. It imposes no measurability assumption on the subset.

hull construction on pp. 116–117 The local erratum has no correction at book

`is_product_measure` gives an abstract characterisation: the Cartesian product
is the largest measure bounded on each measurable rectangle A × B by
alpha(A) beta(B). `product_measure_unique` proves uniqueness from those laws.
The separate `is_product_cover_infimum` records the defining countable-cover
infimum, without choosing an approximation sequence.

`ProductMeasureConstruction.v` proves existence using the project's fixed-family
countable-cover envelope. Internally, inadmissible rectangle pairs have infinite
cost, which does not change the infimum over measurable covers. The witness is
hidden by `ProductMeasureSealed.v`. Public `product_measure` exposes only proved
laws: it is a measure, satisfies the rectangle bound, is the greatest measure
with that bound, and satisfies the covering infimum specification.

`product_measure_sequence_cover_bound` and
`product_measure_sequence_cover_greatest` give the literal sequential infimum
of pp. 114–115. `countable_zero_cost_enumeration` assigns each member of a
countable cover its injective natural code and fills gaps with empty rectangles;
the numerical grouping theorem proves that this preserves the cost. This
includes an empty covering family and allows repeated rectangles in arbitrary
sequence covers. Indices start at zero instead of the printed positive integers.

The measure laws are the only hypotheses on the two factors; finite total
measure and countable measurability are not assumed. Three regression examples
check zero times infinity, infinity times zero, and a null first-factor whole
space against an unrestricted second factor. The exact rectangle equality,
regularity and Fubini conclusions belong to 2.6.2 and are not asserted by this
item's rectangle bound.

`lone_functional_representation_by_finite_decomposition` constructs a measurable
real density for every bounded linear functional on total real L1 functions,
assuming precisely the disjoint finite-measure covering condition printed on
pp. 100–101. Test sets need not be measurable, and the ambient space need
not be countably measurable. The density has essential bound at most the
operator seminorm; countably measurable support is not imposed.

The proof constructs the local functionals by characteristic cutoffs, proves
the actual restricted-integral identity (including partial domains and signed
extended values), applies 2.5.7 on each finite piece, and replaces each local
density by a bounded representative. An opaque disjoint-gluing interface
assembles the density. Measurability is proved by finite Caratheodory tests.
Countable support covers for individual L1 functions, finite prefix cutoffs,
Daniell continuity, and positive/negative parts establish the global integral
formula. It is not merely a specification of the desired pairing.

`radon_measure_finite_decomposition` proves the final paragraph of p. 101,
and `radon_lone_functional_representation` gives its representation corollary.
A sealed maximal-disjoint-family witness is applied to compact sets whose
nonempty intersections with open sets have positive measure. The proof includes
compact-restriction Radon regularity, the support construction for a positive
compact remainder, countability of the family meeting each compact set, and
the maximality contradiction. Arbitrary finite-measure test sets are covered
using compact approximations of a Borel hull. A finite-measure open neighborhood
is obtained directly by Radon outer approximation; compact closure is unnecessary
for the bound used in this implementation.

`continuum_borel_regular_finite_decomposition` and
`continuum_borel_lone_functional_representation` prove the separate CH paragraph
on p. 101. `continuum_hypothesis` is a proposition saying that the power-set
cardinal of the naturals is the successor of the countable cardinal, and remains
an **explicit theorem hypothesis**, never a project axiom. The proof codes the
finite-measure closed family into a continuum representative with countable
prefixes, then subtracts each member's predecessors. It proves measurability,
disjointness and the covering property, using 2.2.3 for closed approximations.

Seven regression examples cover arbitrary counting measure, an empty
zero-measure decomposition, the empty restriction's support, eligibility for
the zero measure, and first/later pieces in a repeated family. All new named
declarations have source comments. The gluing and maximal-family constructions
remain behind opaque interfaces.

The complete corrected item is proved by
`dominated_lattice_integral_representation`. For a monotone Daniell functional
F represented by an L-regular measure, a countably measurable ambient space,
and a Daniell functional A satisfying |A(f)| <= F(|f|), it constructs a
measurable real density k with the actual finite integral formula
A(f) = integral(f k) for every f in L. The density belongs to total L-infinity,
has essential norm at most one, and is almost unique among **all measurable
representing densities**, without a bound or countable-support premise on a
competing density. Neither finite total measure nor closure of L under
negation is added.

The extension follows the corrected positive-cone argument in Federer's
erratum, p. 2, book p. 100 lines 21–24. Subtracting min(f,g) first proves the
L1 Lipschitz estimate on L. Actual L1 approximation by L-positive functions
comes from 2.5.3. An abstract scalar Lipschitz extension is implemented using
an infimum construction, sealed by opaque module ascription, and its
additivity and positive homogeneity are derived from density and uniqueness.
This infimum implementation supplies the continuous extension asserted by
the book; it is not attributed to Federer as his printed construction.
Positive differences then give a linear extension to all total L1 functions,
with the original domination bound and agreement on L. This second existence
construction is also sealed. Applying 2.5.7(ii) gives the density and its norm
bound. Downstream representation proofs use only the exposed laws.

Almost uniqueness is proved separately, so it does not inherit a gratuitous
boundedness restriction from L-infinity duality. Clipping the positive-lattice
approximations below a fixed lattice function and extracting an almost
convergent subsequence gives an integrable majorant for products with an
arbitrary competing density. Dominated convergence localizes zero pairings
to measurable characteristic pieces. Testing the positive-sign piece shows
each absolute product has zero integral. The positive-lattice series cover
from 2.5.3 and a countable conull intersection identify the density globally.

References: Federer 2.5.9, printed p. 100; the local LaTeX erratum, p. 2, book
p. 100 lines 21–24 they are not described as an identical statement of 2.5.9.

Regression examples check the zero functional on a general regular lattice
and the identification of distinct points at zero pseudometric distance.
Every named declaration has a source comment. The two construction modules
remain behind sealed interfaces. The integrated item checkpoint is recorded below; full subsection release verification remains pending.

`lp_linear_functional_representation` proves all three alternatives of
Federer 2.5.7, pp. 98–99: finite conjugate exponents, p=1 with countably
measurable X, and p=infinity with increasing continuity. The functional is
Daniell; an actual total Lq density represents it by the signed integral on
every total Lp test, its Lq norm equals the operator seminorm, and it is
almost everywhere unique. The endpoint assumptions are separate conditional
hypotheses; finite total measure is never imposed. Countably measurable
nonzero loci are retained at infinity, as in 2.4.12.

`is_lp_functional_density` is the abstract integral specification.
`LpFunctionalDensity` derives norm and uniqueness consequences independently
of the existence proof. The operator seminorm is the supremum over the closed
unit ball, including the zero space and zero seminorm. This implements
Federer's erratum p. 2, book p. 98 lines 10–11 and 14, and p. 99 lines 13, 14
and 17. Source comments distinguish supporting lemmas from printed claims and
cite.

The proof develops finite-p increasing continuity, positive and negative
variations, the dominated-density family, finite-Lq and dyadic-average
maximization, and the residual-measure separation argument. It proves actual
maximizers and admissible patches, rather than assuming a maximal density.
Fatou supplies the one-sided pairing inequality needed for increasing closure;
the real lower-limit replacement is proved measurable, countably supported,
and Lq-bounded. The separating lower limit belongs to Lp by domination by the
original test, without an unrestricted lower-limit closure assumption.

The exact norm and almost uniqueness use the previously proved 2.4.16 duality.

The names Daniell integral and monotone Daniell integral refer to the exact
four-law specifications introduced for 2.5.5 and 2.5.1–2.5.2. The subsequent
references to 2.5.7, 2.5.12 and 2.5.13 are previews of applications.

`LatticeAdditiveFunctional` isolates additivity alone and proves zero value,
reflection of each positive order interval by k ↦ f-k, and the resulting
transfer of real upper bounds to -mu. `LatticeExtendedVariation` defines
mu+ by the sealed supremum and mu- by the negative sealed infimum, proves
the equivalent dual supremum formula, and proves the exact finiteness
equivalence on L+. Neither a bound, homogeneity, nor continuity is assumed;
both variations may be infinite.

`VectorFunctionLattice` specifies the additional vector-space closure laws.
`VectorVariationFiniteBounds` uses the positive and negative parts for one
inequality and independent k,l in [0,f], with |k-l| <= f, for the reverse
inequality. `VectorVariationSupremum` proves the exact displayed formula
mu+(f)+mu-(f)=sup{|mu(g)|:g in L, |g| <= f}, including infinite variations.
The outer absolute value is present in Federer's printed formula. Negation
closure and additive negation explain this equivalence.

`VariationIntegralMeasure` names the sum of the real positive and negative
functionals and the sum of their representing measures. It proves the sum
measure laws, allowing infinite masses, and measurability of any set measurable
for both component measures. The alternative notations for mu(f) on p. 98
are typographical conventions rather than additional mathematical assertions.

Three regressions cover zero functionals (including empty X), simultaneous
infinite variations, and negative evaluation on a one-point space, whose
absolute supremum at f=1 equals one. These also distinguish additivity alone
from the bounded Daniell hypotheses of 2.5.5.

`DaniellIntegralUP` states precisely the four hypotheses: additivity, positive
homogeneity, a finite upper bound on each positive order interval, and ordinary
convergence of the functional values when the input functions increase to a
member of L. The functional itself is not required to be monotone.

`DaniellVariationUP` specifies the finite positive and negative variations by
their order laws. The positive variation is the supremum of the values on
0 <= k <= f, k in L; the negative variation is the positive variation of -mu.
`DaniellVariationOrderFormulas` recovers the exact displayed supremum and
negative-infimum formulas using the project's abstract extended bounds.
`DaniellVariationConstruction` proves existence from the bound and zero member;
`DaniellVariationSealed` hides that witness behind opaque module ascription.
All subsequent laws use the abstract specification.

`DaniellIntegralAlgebra`, `DaniellIntegralNegation` and `DaniellVariationBounds`
prove finiteness for -mu, the identity mu(f)=mu+(f)-mu-(f) on L+, positivity,
and order preservation of the variations. `DaniellVariationAddition` uses
Federer's split k=min(f,h), h-k for h <= f+g; `DaniellVariationScaling` includes
the zero scalar; `DaniellVariationIncreasing` passes to the limit in
mu(min(h_n,k)) for each k <= g. `DaniellVariationFunctionals` collects all four
monotone Daniell laws on L+.

`DaniellVariationRepresentation` applies 2.5.2–2.5.3 to both variations to
obtain L+ regular measures. `DaniellSignedVariationIntegral` proves the signed
formula for **every f in L**, using f+ and f-=f+-f and actual finite integral
values for both measures. It does not assume that L is a vector space. the
additional scope here follows Federer.

`DaniellVariationApproximation` chooses g_n in L+ with g_n <= f and
mu(g_n) tending to mu+(f). `DaniellVariationIntegralErrors` proves the two
printed L1 identities and bounds by mu+(f)-mu(g_n), hence convergence of both
errors to zero. `MeasureScalarL1Convergence` uses the exponent-one superlevel
estimate of 2.4.12. `MeasureConvergentSubsequence` applies 2.3.10 twice,
identifying the prescribed limits using uniqueness in measure and preserving
the first almost-everywhere limit through the second extraction.

`BoundedRealFunctionLowerLimit` proves that 0 <= k_n <= f forces a finite
pointwise lower limit everywhere, including exceptional points. The theorem
`daniell_variation_separating_lower_limit` supplies one sequence k_n in L+
below f and one total real h=liminf k_n, with h=f almost everywhere for the
positive measure and h=0 almost everywhere for the negative measure. The
additional global bound 0 <= h <= f is proved. Membership h in L is neither
assumed nor asserted. `daniell_integral_representation` packages every conclusion.

The regular representation requires **L nonempty**. The printed lattice
closure definition permits empty L, for which every functional hypothesis is
vacuous but no L+ regular measure exists: even the empty set has no F2 hull.
This local boundary correction is separate from Federer's erratum. Regressions
cover this obstruction and negative evaluation, a Daniell integral that fails
the monotonicity required in 2.5.2.

sign slip. Its following display and Federer's proof use f-g. This is an
error. The local Federer erratum contains no correction to pp. 96–97.

For every representing measure, `FunctionLatticeLevelRightLimit.v`
proves the displayed formula for phi{f>t}, with f in L+ and t>0.
The limit ranges over all positive real widths tending to zero; the
proof first establishes convergence for reciprocal widths, then uses
monotonicity and cofinality. Positive homogeneity places the width's
inverse outside the functional exactly as in the book.

`FunctionLatticeLevelFamilies.v` defines F0, F1 and F2 exactly as in
Federer, with natural indices starting at zero. Measurability,
finiteness of F0/F2, agreement of every pair of representing measures
on these families, and independence of F2 from the chosen representation
are proved separately. The finite mass of the first set in an F2
sequence is retained. The later examples of nonuniqueness referred to
in the opening sentence belong to 2.5.4 and 2.5.14.

`FunctionLatticeRegularityUP.v` specifies Federer's finite-hull definition of
L regularity. Suiting sequences give F1 outer approximations; finite prefix
intersections and arbitrarily small errors give equal-mass F2 hulls.
`RegularDaniellRepresentation.v` proves regularity of the sealed measure from
2.5.2 and its uniqueness among regular representing measures. An order-bound
argument replaces the printed double-limit extraction, without weakening the
hull conclusion. Every representing measure is dominated by the regular one.
`RegularDaniellEnvelope.v` derives the suiting-envelope law from the abstract
regularity and representation laws, so downstream statements need not mention
a construction.

`RegularDaniellConsequences.v` exposes all four further conditions:

- `regular_daniell_inner_supremum`: for a finite measurable A, its measure is
the supremum of the measures of K={h>=1} minus E contained in A, where h
belongs to L+ and E belongs to F1. The difference-set family is retained
exactly. the quantitative bound at most 3 delta suffices for Federer's printed
strict 4 delta estimate and the resulting supremum law. -
`regular_daniell_positive_l1_density`: for a nonnegative summable partial
function u and delta>0, some h in L+ has integral |h-u| < 4 delta. The proof
supplies a finite integral witness for the error. Suiting sequences and
increasing convergence approximate characteristic functions, and the
integral's finite-simple supremum reduces the general case to finite positive
combinations. This makes explicit the final reduction in Federer, while.
Definedness and nonnegativity are required only almost everywhere; exceptional
infinite values and partial domains remain allowed. -
`regular_daniell_countably_finite_iff`: T has an arbitrary countable cover by
finite-measure sets exactly when a series of functions in L+ dominates its
characteristic function. The series itself may have infinite sum; the empty
covering family is included. - `regular_daniell_measurability_iff`: a subset B
is measurable exactly when B intersect {f>=1} is measurable for every f in L+.
Finite test sets are covered by countably many such levels, and the
infinite-test case follows from the measure splitting inequality.

The empty-lattice boundary is explicit. Closure under the five operations
in 2.5.1 permits empty L, and 2.5.2 still has a vacuous representation.
However F0, F1 and F2 are all empty, so no measure can be L regular:
even the empty set would need an F2 hull. Thus regular existence in
2.5.3 requires a nonempty lattice. This is a local boundary clarification,
not an entry in Federer's erratum. Abstract regularity itself implies
nonemptiness, so the four public consequences need no extra premise.

`SequenceLimitCounterexample.v` proves the final independence assertion
on the exact lattice of all convergent real sequences. The sequence-limit
functional is specified abstractly, supplied by an existence proof and
hidden behind `SequenceLimitFunctionalSealed.v`. It is additive, positively
homogeneous and monotone, but fails increasing continuity: finite-prefix
indicators increase pointwise to one while their functional values remain
zero. Indices start at zero, corresponding to the book's positive integers
by n+1. No weakened finite-support substitute for the lattice is used.

Regressions check failure of regularity for the empty lattice even with
zero measure, the prefix/constant-one jump of the sealed limit functional,
and the density theorem for a function with empty but conull domain under
zero measure.

7249 results in the cumulative audit log across 6962 labelled sections: 922 closed and 6327 using only the three permitted boolp assumptions. Every new declaration was freshly audited; preceding labelled entries retain their previously checked assumption sets.

`MonotoneDaniellRepresentation.v` states the full representation theorem:
every additive, positively homogeneous, monotone and increasing-continuous
real functional on Federer's function lattice has a representing measure,
and every lattice member is summable for that measure. The lattice need
not be a vector space, contain the constant one, or be nonempty. Functions
are total and finite real-valued; the ambient functional's values outside
the lattice remain irrelevant.

The proof follows Federer 2.5.2, printed pp. 92–93.

- Specify suiting sequences, their limiting costs by supremum laws, and the
set function by infimum laws. Prove existence and hide the witness behind
`DaniellCoverSealed.v`.
- Prove the measure laws using finite diagonal sums
over a sealed exhaustion of an arbitrary countable indexing set, followed by
the summable-error selection made explicit. This supplies the full
countable-cover formulation of the project's measure definition.
- Prove the
supported-minorant bound, construct the printed lattice cutoff, and split a
suiting sequence into its minimum with the cutoff and its positive remainder.
Passing both increasing costs through their bound gives level separation and
hence measurability by the previously proved 2.3.2(7).
- Compare consecutive
truncation layers with weighted characteristic functions, telescope their
functional values and integrals, and pass first to unbounded truncation levels
and then to a zero lower cutoff. The latter limit uses the cofinal sequence
1/(n+1). Infinite candidate integrals remain allowed until equality with the
finite functional value proves summability.
- Pass to signed lattice members
by f-minus=f-plus-f, using only ordered subtraction, and expose the
representing measure through `DaniellMeasureUP.v`, a proved existence
construction, and `DaniellMeasureSealed.v`.

The empty case is explicit: the raw infimum of suiting-sequence costs is
infinite even on the empty set when the lattice is empty, so that formula
cannot supply a measure in this case. The representation assertion itself is
vacuous and has the zero measure as a witness. it is a boundary clarification
rather than an entry in Federer's erratum. The public specification requires
the envelope formula only for a nonempty lattice, retaining it for the
following 2.5.3 regularity discussion. No uniqueness of arbitrary representing
measures is asserted.

Natural sequences are indexed from zero, with the corresponding empty
finite-sum base case. The May 1987 erratum has no correction to 2.5.2; the
preceding lattice maximum uses its p. 2 correction to book p. 91 line 28.

Regressions cover representation on the empty lattice, failure of the raw
empty-lattice envelope to be a measure, evaluation on a positive cone that
is not closed under negation, and the zero functional forcing zero total
mass for the envelope measure.

`FunctionLatticeUP.v` specifies Federer's lattice by its five closure laws:
addition, nonnegative scaling, binary minimum, minimum with a nonnegative
constant, and subtraction of ordered pairs. The functions are total and finite
real-valued. The definition permits an empty lattice and does not impose
vector-space structure. `FunctionLatticeLaws.v` derives zero from a member,
unrestricted binary maxima, both parts, and the positive sublattice. The
maximum assertion incorporates Federer's erratum p. 2, book p. 91 line 28: its
sign restriction is removed. The proof uses f+(g-min(f,g)), whose difference
is ordered even when the maximum is negative. the formalisation follows
corrected Federer.

`ContinuousFunctionLattice.v` verifies the all-functions and continuous-
functions examples for arbitrary source topology. `SummableRealFunctionLattice.v`
verifies the summable-functions example using the project's integral
arithmetic and domination results. The constant truncation need not itself
be summable; its nonnegative value gives |min(f,c)|<=|f|. No finite-total-
measure assumption is introduced.

`MonotoneDaniellIntegralUP.v` states the four functional laws in the final

L; existence is proved separately and hidden by an opaque seal.
`RealIntegralFunctional.v` exposes the map and proves additivity, positive
homogeneity, order preservation and increasing continuity on every
summable sublattice. Increasing continuity for signed summable functions
is reduced to 2.4.7 by subtracting the first term. Off-L values of an
ambient representative are unspecified and irrelevant to all laws.

`MeasureJensen.v` supplies the full inequality for an abstract real Banach
space, a closed convex set C, a nonnegative convex function F continuous
only on C, a finite positive measure, and an L1 partial function g with
conull inverse image of C. `measure_jensen_integral_values` proves both
membership of the normalised vector integral in C and the extended-real
inequality for arbitrary integral witnesses. `measure_jensen` additionally
provides existence of both integrals, using their sealed interfaces.
The scalar integral may equal positive infinity. Null modifications retain
the original partial-domain convention. F is represented on the ambient
space, but all continuity, convexity and sign hypotheses concern only C;
its off-C values on the exceptional null set are irrelevant.

The proof follows the three stages in the book. `FiniteImageJensen.v`
derives the finite-image inequality from actual fibre masses summing to
the total mass. Convex translation handles sets that do not contain zero,
and the finite convex inequality is proved using the epigraph.
`ClosedSetFiniteApproximationUP.v` specifies Borel finite-image C-valued
approximants with pointwise convergence and the reference-point error
bound. The existence proof uses a separable closed range, the sealed
Borel retraction and nearest finite-prefix approximation; its witness is
hidden by `ClosedSetFiniteApproximationSealed.v`. The error bound proves
L1 convergence. `BoundedRangeJensen.v` applies finite-image Jensen and
bounded convergence, deriving mean membership from closedness and the
scalar limit from relative continuity. This supporting stage permits
unbounded C: the integrable reference-point majorant replaces the printed
bounded-C premise, while F remains bounded.

`ConvexClosedSublevels.v` proves that the simultaneous norm and F sublevels
are closed and convex. The final exhaustion uses the same sublevel family
as Federer, with cofinal thresholds containing a fixed point c of C.
One implementation difference is explicit: `JensenSublevelApproximation.v`
puts c outside each inverse-image set, keeping the source measure and its
normalising mass fixed instead of normalising restricted measures.
The added complement contribution vanishes in L1, dominated by |g-c|.
When the scalar integral is finite, F(g_n) is dominated by F(g)+F(c), so
its integrals converge too. The infinite-integral case is separate;
mean membership follows from the already proved bounded case with F=0.
This changes the bookkeeping of the printed final limit, not its
hypotheses or inequality, and introduces no finite-dimensional restriction.

Sources: Federer 2.4.19, printed p. 91; May 1987 erratum p. 2, book p. 91
lines 10–11 (L1 notation and proof heading) The finite convex-combination
background is independently expanded in its 10.5(3), printed pp. 97–98; it
does not state the measure-theoretic inequality.

Regressions check a possibly nonzero singleton C with F unrestricted off
C, retention of mean membership when the scalar integral is positive
infinity, and impossibility of a conull empty range at positive mass.

`MeasureExponentDomain.v` joins the finite-p and essential-norm interfaces
into one extended-exponent family. Its domain `1 <= p <= infinity` is
inherited from 2.4.12; the reciprocal domain is `[0,1]`, with zero mapped
to infinity explicitly rather than to the field inverse of zero.
`ExtendedExponentInterval.v` proves that P, the finite-norm exponent set,
is an interval of the extended real line. Empty P and the singleton
infinity exponent are allowed. `MeasureReciprocalInterval.v` proves that
U, the positive finite norms in reciprocal coordinates, is an interval
and a convex set. The common zero-norm criterion identifies the null
nonzero locus independently of the admissible exponent.

`MeasureLogarithmicConvexity.v` proves convexity of `u |-> log(phi_(1/u)(f))`
on U, including `u=0`. Zero weights and equal coordinates are treated
explicitly. Supporting interpolation estimates retain arbitrary normed targets
and partial domains. The preliminary interval proofs use total real functions;
`MeasurePartialReciprocalInterval.v` and `MeasureExponentAlmostEquality.v`
transfer them to conull partial domains. The logarithmic-convexity theorem
therefore retains partial f. `ConvexFunctionUP.v` gives the ordinary convex
function laws independently of any implementation of a domain or map.

`ProbabilityExponentMonotone.v` proves the final assertion for measures
of total mass one, with infinite norm values and both infinite-exponent
endpoints included. Allowing equal exponents harmlessly strengthens the
printed strict ordering. For finite exponents the proof applies Holder
to one and the powered norm; at infinity it compares with the constant
essential bound. The supporting statements work for arbitrary normed
partial functions.

`MeasureExponentContinuity.v` proves the total-function case and
`MeasurePartialExponentContinuity.v` transfers it to conull partial domains,
proving actual subspace continuity on
`Closure P`, with the project's extended-real order topology as both
domain and codomain. Finite limiting exponents use dominated convergence
between finite-norm bracket endpoints. Combining the two absolute-value
regions into `|f|^s + |f|^t` allows arbitrary convergent sequences;
Fatou's inequality handles infinite limiting norms. Positive powers,
varying roots, exponential and reciprocal limits are derived from the
existing scalar specifications and order laws. At infinity the rooted
superlevel estimate supplies the lower bound, while the explicit
logarithmic interpolation chord supplies the upper bound. Zero and
infinite essential norms are included.

General topology makes the closure assertion explicit: a sealed
countable base for the extended real line supplies the sequential
criterion, closed neighborhoods pass relative limits to continuity on
the entire closure, and interval structure identifies the closure of
the finite exponents with `Closure P` whenever a finite member exists.
The remaining empty/singleton-infinity case is proved separately.
The private rational-interval enumeration is imported only by its seal
and the assumption audit. No external integration, Lp interpolation or
norm-continuity theorem is imported.

`MeasureDualTestUP.v` specifies full Lebesgue-space membership and norm at
most one. This follows Federer's erratum, p. 2, book p. 89 line 19, which
replaces the printed equality-one condition. The value sets retain all defined
signed integrals in Federer's display; no value is assigned to an undefined
difference of infinities. The second value set requires literal equality of
the signs, including domains. our upper bounds cover every defined signed
value and our lower witnesses have nonnegative products.

`DualPowerTestAdmissible.v` proves that the printed power test
(|f|/phi_p(f))^(p-1) sign(f) is a full Lq member, has norm one,
preserves the sign and attains the input norm for finite p>1 and positive
finite norm. At p=1, sign(f) attains the extended L1 norm, including
zero and infinity; this strengthens the printed positive finite-norm
attainment clause. The zero finite-p case uses a norm-zero sign test.

`PositiveIntegrableWeightUP.v` and its sealed implementation provide a
weight positive on every point of a prescribed countably measurable set,
including null pieces, bounded by one with integral at most one. The
private construction uses a countable finite-measure cover and positive
geometric coefficients divided by one plus the measure. Weighted signs
therefore give full-sign unit tests for every finite q>=1. This avoids
discarding null parts of the nonzero locus when choosing the weighted
partition in the printed proof.

For p=infinity, positive superlevels contain measurable subsets of finite
positive measure. Normalized signed characteristic tests have L1 norm one and
give the required lower integral bound. A convex mixture with a full-sign unit
test restores the sign everywhere while retaining an arbitrarily large
proportion of that bound. This proves Federer's unqualified sign restriction
also at infinite essential norm

`BoundedMeasureApproximationUP.v` specifies measurable bounded approximants
with finite-measure support, increasing absolute values and pointwise
convergence. Its sealed witnesses retain f on increasing bounded pieces of a
finite-measure cover and vanish elsewhere; they are eventually equal to f at
every point. Fatou lower semicontinuity forces their Lp norms to be unbounded
when phi_p(f) is infinite. No external Lp or measure-duality theorem is
imported.

Sources: Federer 2.4.16, printed pp. 89–90 The local scan was checked; a

preserve the unit bound. Regression examples exercise nonzero constants under

counterexample.

`RealFunctionalNormUP.v` specifies the dual norm as the least nonnegative
bound on evaluations. Continuity supplies a finite bound, and the private
unit-ball supremum construction proves existence. An opaque seal exposes
only the proved laws. `RealFunctionalUnitBall.v` proves equivalence with
Federer's precise signed unit-ball least-upper-bound formula, without
assuming attainment or finite dimension.

`ContinuousDualUP.v` specifies a normed dual by its injective linear
evaluation map, continuous image, representation of every continuous
functional, and the least-bound norm laws. `ContinuousDualNorm.v` proves
that those laws make the supplied function a norm. A private subspace
construction and opaque seal establish existence. Applying the same
interface again supplies the normed bidual.

`ContinuousBidualUP.v` characterises the canonical map by evaluation and
defines reflexivity as its surjectivity. The canonical map has a proved
existence witness behind an opaque seal. `ContinuousBidualIsometry.v`
proves its norm identity using Hahn–Banach norming functionals.
`FiniteAlgebraicBidual.v` proves algebraic bidual representation from a
finite dual basis; `FiniteContinuousReflexivity.v` uses the existing
linear-extension theorem to prove surjectivity for every finite-dimensional
normed space, including dimension zero. The algebraic supporting lemmas
hold over arbitrary fields; the normed statements retain real scalars.

`MeasureBidualIntegral.v` packages sigma with the exact scalar integral
evaluations, norm bound, addition and scalar laws, Lipschitz estimate in
the L1 seminorm, and image inclusion for every L1 function. The formal
proof order differs from the printed order: finite-image approximation
and sequential closedness first establish the integral representation;
sigma is then identified as tau composed with that integral. Thus the
printed maps and conclusions are proved without introducing a bidual
representation assumption or importing an external vector-integral theorem.
The partial-domain convention is retained; linear laws use common domains.
The total-domain vector-space correction remains as recorded in the
preceding continuations.

with an empty basis. Principal declarations and specification fields are
included in the assumption audit.

The proof implements Federer's closed-subspace argument sequentially.
`MeasureLpFiniteApproximationSequence.v` gives a sequence of finite-image Lp
approximants for any original partial-domain input. Symmetry and the triangle

The sealed finite-image integral supplies integral witnesses, and the

`MeasureFunctionalIntegralDifference.v` proves the scalar difference estimate
for integral alpha(f) without assuming vector-integral existence.
`MeasureFunctionalIntegralConvergence.v` uses it to prove continuity of these

the scalar integrals converge both to integral alpha(f) and, by continuity
of alpha, to alpha(y) for the limiting vector y. Uniqueness of real limits
identifies them for every continuous linear functional. Thus the limit
satisfies the full vector-integral specification.

`MeasureVectorIntegralConstruction.v` combines finite-image density,

proof. Its chosen approximation sequence and limit are hidden behind

function is zero under arbitrary ambient measure, and that the empty-domain
function has a zero chosen integral under null measure.

This continuation completes the finite-p seminorm laws, Lp completeness and

`MeasureLoneTriangle.v` proves the p=1 triangle inequality from the integral
of the pointwise norm inequality. `MeasureLpTriangle.v` combines it with
Minkowski and handles infinite input norms, giving subadditivity on all of A.
`MeasureLpNormTotal.v` packages nonnegativity, absolute homogeneity and the
triangle inequality as `is_extended_seminorm` on the actual subspace of total
measurable normed functions. This follows the erratum p. 1, book p. 86 bottom;
it does not change Federer's partial-domain A or Lp definitions.

`MeasureLpComplete.v` proves full finite-p Lp completeness, including p=1,
for a target supplied with the abstract completeness and norm laws. The
quantitative gauge bound turns an Lp Cauchy sequence into a Cauchy sequence
in measure. The existing 2.3.10 theorem gives an almost-everywhere convergent
subsequence in A. Lower semicontinuity bounds each error by its lower limit,
proving norm convergence. Addition closure proves the limit has finite norm;
finite norm then supplies the required countable measurability. Intermediate
results allow infinite individual norms in the Cauchy-to-A-limit statement.

`MeasureLpFiniteImageDensity.v` proves that every partial-domain Lp function
has a finite-image Lp approximant within any prescribed positive error.
The proof works for arbitrary normed targets, without requiring completeness,
finite dimension or finite source measure. Total representatives and almost-
equality invariance transfer the result back to the book's partial functions.

The density proof makes the finite-partition step explicit. Abstract
`FiniteNearestApproximationUP.v` laws specify a Borel nearest label in each
finite prefix of a supplied candidate sequence. Private recursion proves
existence and is hidden by a sealed module. A second sealed witness supplies
a dense sequence with a prescribed first point. On a closed separable
essential range, a Borel retraction and the nearest partitions yield finite
Borel approximants. Including zero as first candidate bounds the error by
the original norm, so |f|^p dominates every powered error. The project's
2.4.9 bounded-convergence theorem and positive-root continuity make the Lp
errors tend to zero. Every approximant satisfies A membership, finite norm
and countable measurability. No external Lp, density or integration theorem
is imported; downstream proofs use only the exposed approximation laws.

The nearest-prefix choice is a supporting implementation of finite partitions
with a common integrable majorant; it is not attributed to Federer.
Regressions cover L1 density for an empty-domain function under a null
measure, including zero-dimensional targets, and exact preservation of zero by
the finite nearest approximants despite repeated labels or ties.

Remaining claims in 2.4.12: finite-image density at p=infinity for finite-
dimensional targets, and vector-integral existence, linearity, norm bound,
and the reflexivity discussion. The earlier scalar separated-sequence
obstruction is not a complete measure-space counterexample.

`MeasureMinkowski.v` proves the finite-p inequality for the book's partial-domain
Lp membership, for every p>1 and arbitrary normed targets. The proof first
establishes that the sum belongs to Lp, using the printed bound by
2^p times the sum of the powered norms. The measurable scalar weight
|f+g|^(p-1) has the required conjugate norm. Two applications of the project's
Hölder theorem give the powered inequality, followed by cancellation; the
zero sum-norm case is included. No external Minkowski or Lp theorem is used.

`MinkowskiEqualityUP.v` specifies the printed equality condition using one pair
of nonnegative coefficients summing to one and a conull common domain.
`MeasureMinkowskiEquality.v` proves both directions. Sufficiency follows
from almost-everywhere norm invariance and homogeneity. For necessity, equality
forces both intermediate Hölder bounds to be attained; equality in Young's
inequality gives the normalised power identities, and cancellation gives the
printed norm proportions. Separate proofs include either zero input norm.

`PositiveProportionalityUP.v` specifies the inner-product condition with one
global nonnegative coefficient and either orientation.
`MeasureMinkowskiInnerEquality.v` proves its equivalence with equality in
Minkowski. The existing strict triangle inequality from 1.7.1 forces pointwise
dependence; scalar norm identities force the multiplier to be nonnegative. The
fixed norm coefficients determine the same vector multiplier at all points. A
zero second coefficient gives the reverse orientation with multiplier zero.
The converse follows from homogeneity for arbitrary norms. The inner product
and its norm are supplied by their abstract laws; no finite-dimensional or
completeness assumption is added.

Supporting results include addition closure of finite Lp for p>=1,
almost-everywhere norm invariance including infinite values, and equality
in the norm-product Hölder comparison for arbitrary normed targets.
Regressions exercise empty partial domains under a null measure for every
normed target (including zero dimension), and the reverse orientation with
zero proportionality coefficient under an arbitrary measure.

Item checkpoint: Federer 2.4.14 (2026-10-17 UTC)
Switch: RP.2026.02.0~9.1~2026.01, explicitly selected for every command.

The integrated make -j1 build, full recursive make -j1 check of all eight
roots, and fresh per-item Print Assumptions audit passed without warnings under the

`MeasureHolder.v` states the complete finite-conjugate-exponent theorem for
Federer's partial-domain Lp membership: the real product is summable, the
absolute integral is at most the product of the two norms, and equality
holds exactly when the signed powers are almost linearly dependent.
`MeasureSignedPowerDependenceUP.v` specifies the latter condition by one
real coefficient, in either orientation, on a conull set. The coefficient
and its orientation do not depend on the point.

its integral first establishes summability of the norm product. Positive
finite norms permit normalisation, and vanishing norms imply almost-everywhere
vanishing. The supporting `MeasureHolderNormProduct.v` estimate allows
arbitrary normed targets, as needed in the proof of 2.4.15. The real
signed-product bound follows from the already proved absolute-integral
inequality.

For equality, a nonnegative integrable function has integral zero exactly
when it vanishes almost everywhere. Applying this to an ordered integral
deficit proves equality of the normalised powers. Equality between the
absolute integral and the absolute-value integral forces a single product
sign. Scalar signed-power identities then give the dependence relation;
positive rescaling recovers the original functions. Conversely, taking
absolute values and integrating a dependence relation determines the
coefficient's magnitude. For unit powered integrals it is one, so the two
signs yield product integral one or minus one. Rescaling and integral
uniqueness prove sufficiency. Both orientations and all zero-norm cases
are included. Every step uses the project's own integral and norm laws;
no external Holder or Lp result is imported.

The core estimates assume measurability and finite norms, which is slightly
stronger in scope than the printed Lp statement. The final public theorem uses
the book's Lp membership predicate, including its A and countable-
measurability conditions. The exponents are finite real numbers. Federer's
printed p,q >= 1 and reciprocal identity force p,q > 1, as proved in
`ConjugateExponents.v`. The endpoint estimates needed in 2.4.16 remain part of
that later development.

Regressions check empty partial domains and equality under the null measure,
and opposite unit constants in L2 for a unit counting mass: both norms are
one, the signed product integral is minus one, and the equality criterion
holds. The latter also exercises actual Lp membership and the negative
sign of the dependence coefficient.

Sources: Federer 2.4.14, printed p. 88. it has no corresponding integration
chapter. No relevant entry in Federer's local LaTeX erratum changes this
theorem.

At most two compiler/checker processes were used.

**This item is not complete.** The current development covers the extended
seminorm and Hahn–Banach discussion, the norm definitions, the infinity
endpoint, the finite-p lower-semicontinuity and gauge estimates, and the
abstract vector-integral characterisation. It does not yet prove all the
claims of pp. 86–88. The partial checkpoint passed its integrated build, recursive kernel check,
and fresh audit of every new declaration. Completion of the item and the
clean subsection release verification remain pending.

`ExtendedSeminormUP.v` states the book's possibly infinite seminorm laws,
including zero times infinity equal to zero. The finite locus is a vector
subspace. The Hahn–Banach proof follows the one-vector extension interval
and maximal linear graph argument; the existence witness is private and
sealed. Public extension and norming results use its abstract laws.

The printed norming-functional corollary needs a finiteness hypothesis at the
prescribed vector: a real-valued functional cannot attain infinity.
`ExtendedSeminormNormingCorrection.v` proves this with the seminorm zero at
zero and infinite elsewhere. The corrected norming theorem is proved for every
vector of finite seminorm. This is a local correction, not a correction in
Federer's erratum.

The essential norm is specified by nonnegative null-superlevel thresholds,
with a proved private infimum witness and sealed public interface. The
nonnegative threshold range is explicit: unrestricted real thresholds
would give a negative infimum under the null measure. A counterexample
records that ambiguity separately from the erratum. The public results
include attainment, null norm, comparison, homogeneity, triangle inequality,
the gauge bound, lower semicontinuity, and the extended seminorm on total
functions. Completeness in the measurable normed function space is proved
through 2.3.10 and lower semicontinuity. `MeasureLinfinityCompleteness.v`
proves completeness with every L-infinity membership condition, including
countable measurability of the nonzero locus.

The finite-p norm is specified by the integral of the powered norm and
its positive root, with an existence witness and sealed interface.
Positive powers preserve nonnegative extended order, limits and lower
limits, including zero and infinity. Measurability of the powered norm
is proved from sublevels, using only closed real rays from the narrow
MathComp `num_normedtype` topology adapter. The scalar exponential witness
and real powers are supplied by the separately formalised 2.4.13.

`MeasureLpNormLowerLimit.v` proves finite-p lower semicontinuity by applying
Fatou to the powered norms and commuting the positive root with the scalar
lower limit. It retains exact partial domains; the limit is assumed
measurable, supplied separately by the project's measurable-limit theorem
when used for limits in A. `MeasureNormPowerMarkov.v` proves the displayed
superlevel estimate. `MeasureLpCountableMeasurability.v` proves the
parenthetical redundancy of countable measurability for finite p by the
union of reciprocal norm superlevels. `MeasureLpGaugeBound.v` proves the
quantitative gauge comparison, including zero and infinite integral cases.
These inequalities extend to measurable functions with possibly infinite
norms, beyond their printed finite-norm applications.

Continuous real functionals separate points by the proved norming theorem.
`MeasureVectorIntegralUP.v` specifies vector integrals by all continuous
dual evaluations and proves uniqueness. It does **not** assume or yet prove
existence. `SeparatedSequenceFiniteImage.v` proves the separated-sequence
obstruction used in discussing finite-image density; it is not yet a
complete measure-space counterexample to the uncorrected printed claim.

Federer's erratum, pp. 1–2, book p. 86 lines 26–27 and the bottom of
p. 86, and p. 87 line 1, restricts vector-space/seminorm assertions to
total functions in the corresponding tilde-space. The partial-domain
A and Lp definitions remain partial. The erratum's p. 2 entry for book
p. 87 line 18 restricts finite-image density to p < infinity or finite-
dimensional target. Both conventions are retained in the development;
the corrected density theorem itself remains pending.

Remaining claims in this item: the general finite-p seminorm and its
Lebesgue-space consequences (using 2.4.15 as Federer directs), finite-p
completeness, all cases of the corrected finite-image density theorem,
and vector-integral existence, linearity, the norm bound, and the
reflexivity discussion. Holder and Minkowski are being developed as
prerequisites; completion of their scalar prerequisites does not imply
completion of this item.

Regressions check essential norm zero for every partial function under
a null measure and finite-p norm zero for a total zero function under
an arbitrary measure, including infinite ambient mass.

Sources: Federer 2.4.12, printed pp. 86–88 it has no measure-theory chapter.
The cited density and existence passages identify pending targets, not claims
that their complete formal proofs already exist.

For positive weights summing to one, the arithmetic mean dominates the
weighted geometric mean, with equality exactly when the bases agree. The

`MeasureIntegralAbsoluteContinuity.v` states the complete strict estimate:
for every summable partial function f and positive epsilon, there is a
positive delta such that every measurable A with measure at most delta
has absolute integral over A strictly below epsilon. No finite ambient
measure is required.

The supporting modules define upper truncations retaining the original
partial domain, prove their measurability and increasing convergence,
and apply 2.4.7 to obtain a small integral of the truncation remainder.
The subset estimate bounds the restricted function by the whole remainder
plus a positive multiple of the characteristic function of A. Choosing
ceiling c=n+1 and delta=epsilon/(2c) gives Federer's strict inequality.
This comparison is equivalent to the two restricted terms in his proof;
it does not require constructing a value for an undefined signed integral.

Regressions exercise an empty partial domain under a null measure and a
singleton characteristic function under counting measure on the infinite
ambient space of natural numbers.

Sources: Federer 2.4.11, statement on printed p. 85 and proof on p. 86 the
formal target retains Federer's strict conclusion. No relevant Federer erratum
entry applies.

The zero-extension graph is specified independently, given an existence
witness and sealed. On A it retains f's values and undefined points;
outside A it is zero. `MeasureSubsetIntegral.v` defines abstract subset
integral values and public upper/lower integrals; a signed getter requires
proved integrability. The domain is exactly `(A intersect domain f) union
complement A`, and the relative almost-everywhere domain premise is proved
equivalent to conullness of this zero extension.

`MeasureDensityUP.v` specifies psi(A) as the upper integral of h over A.
For a density nonnegative almost everywhere, `MeasureDensityCover.v`
derives the measure laws through nonnegative upper approximants, countable
sum interchange and summable approximation errors. The density need not
be measurable. `MeasureDensityMeasurable.v` proves preservation of every
original measurable set by splitting upper step functions; the public
`MeasureDensity.v` exposes the sealed instance. Null-set preservation
and transfer of measurable functions are also proved.

`MeasureChangeOfDensity.v` states the identity between integration under
psi and integration of the density product under phi as an equivalence
of integral-value predicates. The product explicitly assigns zero to
zero times either infinity, while retaining the common partial domain.
Measurability of that product is proved via its Borel scalar map. The
proof follows Federer's references to 2.3.3, 2.4.8 and 2.4.4(6):
weighted characteristic functions, countable nonnegative sums, and signed
parts. A sealed harmonic-sequence witness establishes the existence of
the positive vanishing divergent weights required by the expansion.
Null replacements recover original partial domains and an almost-
everywhere nonnegative density. Infinite integral values are permitted;
undefined differences of infinite variations stay undefined on both sides.

`MeasureIntegralSplitting.v` gives the equivalence between an integral
and a defined sum of its two measurable-set restrictions. Finiteness of
one original variation controls the same side of both restricted integrals.
`MeasureIntervalIntegral.v` defines the real and extended-real half-open
interval conventions, and `MeasureIntervalSplitting.v` proves adjacent-
interval additivity with precisely the measurability of the dividing
lower half-line required by Federer. It does not require the two interval
sets to be measurable separately.

Regressions cover a zero integrand under an infinite density on a unit
mass, exercising the special product convention through the complete
density formula, and coincident interval endpoints for an arbitrary
partial function, including an empty domain.

`MeasureBoundedConvergence.v` states the bounded-convergence theorem
with arbitrary partial extended-real measurable functions. A summable
majorant bounds the absolute values almost everywhere, and the sequence
converges almost everywhere in the extended-real order topology. The
conclusions include finite integral values for the sequence, its limit
and the absolute differences, convergence of the error integrals to zero,
and convergence of the signed integral values.

The proof follows Federer's Fatou argument for `2h - |fn-g|`.
Summability gives almost-everywhere finiteness of h. Countably many
bounds and the pointwise limit yield a common conull region of finite
values. Domination implies summability, and integral invariance under
null modifications identifies the lower-limit integral with that of 2h.
The scalar consequence of Fatou is expressed through eventual strict
lower bounds, equivalently to Federer's displayed limsup calculation.
Here 2h is represented by h+h. No external integration theorem is used.

Supporting results include invariance of integral values under almost
equality, absolute summability and domination criteria, finiteness almost
everywhere, and integral differences. Scalar limits retain their abstract
order specification. Exact partial domains, including undefined infinite
subtractions on exceptional sets, are preserved throughout.

Regressions cover empty partial domains under a null measure and a
constant negative sequence on a unit mass, ensuring that neither total
domains nor nonnegative sequence terms are inadvertently required.

error integrals. No relevant erratum entry.

`MeasureCountableNonnegativeSum.v` proves that the integral of a countable
pointwise nonnegative sum is the sum of the integrals. It retains arbitrary
countable index sets, the empty family, partial summand domains and infinite
values. The summands are pointwise nonnegative, as in Federer. Abstract
integral-value and public sealed-sum versions are provided.

The proof uses a sealed increasing finite exhaustion of the countable
index set. Finite integral additivity gives the integrals of the finite
partial sums; §2.4.7 gives their limit. The numerical-sum exhaustion law
of 2.1.1(13) identifies this limit with the sum of the integral values.
The eventual domain of the finite sums is proved to be exactly the common
domain of all summands.

The pointwise sum is specified abstractly by its graph and the existing
numerical-sum laws. Its implementation is sealed. The specification also
handles signed sums at precisely the points where those sums are defined,
as supporting infrastructure; the interchange theorem is proved only
under the stated nonnegativity hypothesis. Undefined sums and points
outside any summand's domain receive no value. In the nonnegative case,
the domain is the full common domain. The empty sum is total and zero.

Regressions cover the empty family on an arbitrary measure space and a
countable family of constant-one functions on a unit mass, whose
pointwise sum and integral are both infinity.

`MeasureIncreasingConvergence.v` proves increasing convergence of the
integrals to the integral of the pointwise limit. It explicitly proves
existence of the pointwise increasing limit on a conull set, integrability
of the limit, and convergence of the integral values, including infinity.
Abstract integral-value and sealed-limit versions are provided.

The proof follows Federer: monotonicity bounds every sequence integral
by the limit's integral, and Fatou's lemma gives the reverse limiting
inequality. The supporting modules identify the lower limit of an
increasing scalar sequence with its supremum and transfer this result
to partial-function graphs. Convergence uses the existing order-interval

the proof. No finiteness of the total measure or of the limiting integral is
required.

characteristic functions of successive infinite tails increase pointwise to
zero. Every integral is minus infinity, whereas the integral of the limit is
zero.

`MeasureFatou.v` states Fatou's lemma for nonnegative measurable
extended-real functions, preserving infinite integral values and partial
domains. It proves integrability of the pointwise lower-limit function
as well as the integral inequality. Nonnegativity may hold almost
everywhere, a null-set extension of the printed hypothesis. Abstract
integral-value and sealed lower-limit formulations are both provided.

The proof follows Federer's finite-family argument. A lower step sum is
bounded by its positive variation, which is tested on finite positive
level sets. Removing a common null exceptional set preserves their
measures. The increasing sets B_i,n exhaust these test sets; finite sums
of weighted characteristic functions bound each sequence integral.
Continuity from below for measures, finite increasing-sum bounds and
the scalar contraction squeeze give the result. No external integration
or measure-convergence theorem is imported.

`ExtendedLowerLimitUP.v`, `ExtendedLowerLimitConstruction.v`,
`ExtendedLowerLimitSealed.v`, and `ExtendedLowerLimit.v` provide the
scalar lower limit through abstract supremum laws and a sealed witness.

tail-set exhaustion, null restriction and scalar limiting steps explicit.

A regression uses the characteristic functions of successive singleton
natural numbers with counting measure. Every integral is one; the
pointwise lower limit is zero with integral zero. Thus Fatou's inequality
can be strict.

The three assertions are stated for arbitrary characteristic maps satisfying
the abstract one/zero laws:

1. A measurable set's characteristic function is integrable, with integral
   equal to the measure of the set, including infinite measure.
2. For every set, its measure lies between the lower and upper integrals
   of its characteristic function.
3. For a regular measure, the upper integral equals the measure of the set.

`MeasureCharacteristicIntegral.v`, `MeasureCharacteristicUpperBound.v`,
`MeasureCharacteristicLowerBound.v`, and `MeasureCharacteristicRegular.v`
contain the principal results. `MeasureAlmostSubsetBound.v` and
`MeasureStepFibreUnion.v` supply the null-exception comparison and the
countable-additivity calculation used in the proofs.

The lower bound uses levels between zero and one and the upper bound levels at
least one. Zero-measure fibres are retained in these countable families; their
summands vanish. The lower comparison passes through the positive variation of
the assigned sum, without imposing finiteness. The regular case uses a
measurable superset of equal measure, as in the source.

Regressions cover the empty characteristic function on an arbitrary
measure space and the infinite integral of the characteristic function
of all natural numbers under counting measure.

Complete all eight assertions, including step-function identification, nonnegative
integrability, nonzero scalar homogeneity in both directions, addition for
all defined extended integral sums, comparison with automatic integrability,
the part-difference criterion, the absolute-value bound, and absolute-value
summability.

The nonnegative proof follows Federer's two cases. Positive measure of the
infinite level yields arbitrarily large characteristic lower functions.
Otherwise geometric cells [t^j,t^(j+1)) produce a lower step function u with
t*u an upper function. Cell coverage is proved locally by an Archimedean
power estimate and first crossing; the scalar quantisation is specified by
laws, constructed privately and sealed. Infinite values are replaced by zero
only inside the approximant, with their nullity checked for comparisons.
The approximant retains f's conull partial domain, whereas Federer assigns
zero also at points outside that domain. This documented null-set variation
preserves the upper/lower comparisons.

The comparison proof uses upper/lower monotonicity with the finite-part
criterion to make the book's implicit integrability conclusions explicit;
this is a documented variation on the printed difference argument.
Addition retains finite, positive-infinite and negative-infinite sums.
Opposite infinities remain undefined. The final arguments identify the
absolute integral with the sum of the two nonnegative part integrals.

Historical verification records follow. Statements below about deferred

Federer, Chapter 1: version 0.17.0 on Rocq 9.1, rebuilt on universal properties
(tensor products, tensor algebra and exterior algebra)
Verification date (UTC): 2026-10-12

Changes since the Rocq 9.1 port (commit eb06d98):
  Tensor products. The tensor product of V_1, ..., V_m is the predicate
    is_tensor_product_family on an m-linear map on {dffun forall i, V i}
    (TensorFamilyUP.v). Uniqueness, induced maps and spanning by m-fold
    products are proved from the predicate alone. The quotient of finitely
    supported functions by the multilinearity relations proves existence
    (TensorFamilyConstruction.v). TensorSealed.v hides it behind an opaque
    module signature; V (x) W is the case of the family (V, W), and the
    binary predicate is equivalent to the case m = 2. The former binary
    construction TensorConstruction.v is removed; TensorProduct.v and
    TensorSpan.v derive the binary interface from the universal property.
  Tensor algebra. is_graded_tensor_algebra (TensorAlgebraUP.v) states
    Federer 1.2.3 as a property: each homogeneous component, with the
    product of generators, is an m-fold tensor product of V. Spanning and
    the algebra universal property is_tensor_algebra are derived from it.
    TensorAlgebraSealed.v hides the word-algebra quotient, which is used
    only to prove existence (WordTensorWitness.v). TensorAlgebra.v,
    TensorGrading.v and TensorPowers.v derive the interface used by later
    files from the property.
  Exterior algebra. is_exterior_power_algebra (ExteriorAlgebraUP.v)
    states Federer 1.3.1 as a property: generator squares vanish, the
    product of m generators has degree m, and each degree-m component has
    the universal property for alternating m-linear maps. Spanning and the
    square-zero universal property is_exterior_algebra are derived from it.
    ExteriorSealed.v hides the quotient of the tensor algebra by mV, which
    is used only to prove existence (ExteriorQuotientWitness.v).
    ExteriorPowers.v proves from the universal properties that the kernel
    of the canonical map is mV and that each power is the quotient of the
    tensor power.
  Later files. Every later file uses only the sealed interfaces. Two
    proofs that relied on computation through the quotient construction
    now use tensor_algebra_extension_word (TensorAlgebraMaps.v) and the
    new lemma exterior_algebra_map_eq (ExteriorMaps.v,
    CharacteristicPolynomial.v). Maps out of the exterior algebra in
    AlternatingExtension.v, AlternatingIsomorphism.v and the exterior
    examples are obtained from the universal property instead of the
    quotient. In exterior_power_is_quotient and in the lemmas about the
    canonical map, the canonical homomorphism is now the algebra map
    exterior_projection given by the universal property of the tensor
    algebra; no other statement of an existing result changed.

Build:
  make clean; make -j8
  Result: exit status 0, in 1094 s.
  All 303 theory files and all four example files compiled from a
  clean tree. The build prints no warning.

Independent kernel check:
  make check, that is
  rocqchk -silent -Q theories Federer -Q tests FedererTests Federer.GrassmannSpaces Federer.TensorFamily FedererTests.Examples FedererTests.GradedExamples FedererTests.SignedExamples FedererTests.ExteriorExamples
  Result: exit status 0, in 121 s.
  The check is recursive. Every one of the 303 theory modules is a

Coverage:
  Federer 1.1.1 is now proved for m factors: universal property,
  uniqueness, existence, naturality and spanning. Federer 1.2.2 and
  1.3.1–1.6.1 retain their completed treatment.
  Each assertion of 1.6.2 is indexed in docs/chapter1.md.

  G(V,m) is the set of actual subspaces with a basis of length m.
  The association from nonzero simple m-vectors is constructed and
  proved surjective by taking the product of a basis of each subspace.
  The fibres are precisely nonzero scalar proportionality classes.
  The proof includes m=0 directly, without invoking the invalid
  degree-zero simplicity/dimension equivalence of 1.6.1.

  Positive scalar proportionality is an equivalence relation and
  refines nonzero proportionality. G_o(V,m), also named G_0, is the
  actual quotient by this relation; equality of classes is equivalent
  to the stated relation, and every class has a representative.
  Both Euclidean abbreviations are defined, retaining the names G
  and G_o (and G_0) in the FedererEuclideanGrassmann namespace.

  The oriented construction is over an ordered real field. The
  association retains the characteristic-different-from-two hypothesis.
  This item constructs sets and equivalence classes, as defined in
  1.6.2; it does not assert manifold topology at this stage.

  Chapter 1 as a whole is not complete. Earlier gaps are listed in
  README.md and docs/section12.md. No completion claim is made for
  1.6.3 or a later numbered item in this release.

Approximation by maps of class k+1, pp. 227–228: if A is a subset of an open set B of R^m, f : B -> R^n is of class k and lim sup ||D^k f(x)-D^k f(a)||/|x-a| is finite at every point a of A, then for every epsilon>0 there is a map g : R^m -> R^n of class k+1 with L^m(A cap {x : f(x)<>g(x)})<epsilon. Conventions: the finite upper limit is a bound M|x-a| on a ball for the actual kth differentials; A need not be measurable; every finite dimension m is included, dimension zero by the constant map f(0). No new private witness is introduced.
